In the words of Francis Bacon: “Truth emerges more readily from error than from confusion.”</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnmee41nbr37"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefmee41nbr37">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This model is illustrative, and adopts a narrow take on ‘context’: at all points, mentors will in all likelihood have good object-level takes that the IC doesn’t due to having better research taste, but this sketch doesn’t account for that.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn751sl4x4a6r"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref751sl4x4a6r">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hofstadter's law: It always takes longer than you expect, even when you take into account Hofstadter's law. Source: </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hofstadter%27s_law" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Wikipedia</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnrfvx8hewr"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefrfvx8hewr">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I often start my writing session </span><a href="https://www.squibler.io/dangerous-writing-prompt-app"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">using this app</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which deletes all my words if I stop typing, to get the momentum going</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Q4rcvgjbgjTwmn8Xa/research-lessons-i-learned-at-mats-and-redwood#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [More people should write basic AI safety explainers](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/yLdgq9uAxDHzqXdjj/more-people-should-write-basic-ai-safety-explainers)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think people continue to underrate the value of writing the clearest, most basic, simplest possible case for important parts of AI safety. For example, before I wrote my own explainer for the </span><a href="https://linch.substack.com/p/simplest-case-ai-catastrophe" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">simplest possible case for AI doom</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, I noticed that all existing explainers, of which there aren't many, had multiple of the six problems below: </span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">They have too many extraneous details</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">They appeal to overly complex analogies</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">They seem to spend much of their time responding to insider debates and come across as shadow-boxing objections.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">They have "meta" stuff prominently in the text.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Often they "sound like science fiction." I think this plausibly was correct historically but in the year 2026 they don't need to be.</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Often they reference too much insider jargon and language.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I set out to improve on the SOTA. I did not fully succeed</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefnpiuuerfjh"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnnpiuuerfjh">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, but I think it was moderately better. Since then, there have been other, probably better, explainers (Yay!).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But actually this is true not just for the core case but many other parts as well. For example, before I wrote </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/RCYF2rW8wgusidZk7/what-is-neuralese-and-why-is-it-bad" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">my explainer on recurrent neuralese</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (which has since been superseded by Scott's </span><a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/the-specter-of-neuralese" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">better explainer</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), the top Google search results for "neuralese" was some combination of a hacker news thread on AI 2027, AI-generated blogs and </span><a href="https://x.com/erikbryn/status/2065142108823150690" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">some tweet</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that confuses more than it enlightens. Oops.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But maybe neuralese isn't just that big a deal? Well we also don't have a good longform post explaining the details/mechanics of how AIs can actually kill everyone (this may sound obvious to ppl in the LW crowd, but it's patently not obvious to the general public</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8cyaletbva8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8cyaletbva8">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">!) </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We also don't have great explanations for </span><a href="https://longerramblings.substack.com/p/what-would-it-mean-to-ban-recursive" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">what RSI even is</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, never mind why and how to ban it. In my own research area, AI superpersuasion, I see many areas of confusion regularly</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefy11lhu26wdb"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fny11lhu26wdb">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. And so forth.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A lot of the time I think people just don't try to explain things clearly and often. In 2019, back when I lived in Silicon Valley, I'd talk to my programmer friends and often see them thinking they understand the case for risk but totally not getting basic concepts. So I'd try to explain what I believe in simple technical concepts that are graspable to programmers. I did it a few times and people quite liked it/were moderately convinced. I asked a seniorish person in AI safety whether it was worth writing it up and they were unconvinced it'd be worth my time. He was skeptical my article would lead to much since it wouldn't move people beyond </span><a href="https://www.vox.com/future-perfect/2018/12/21/18126576/ai-artificial-intelligence-machine-learning-safety-alignment" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Kelsey's explainer</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. I think this is miscalibrated</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref2e5729wt2x7"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn2e5729wt2x7">[4]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Different things convince different people. Kelsey's a better writer than me, and writing to a general audience. I was targeting programmers/technical talent.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If something's sufficiently important, you want "more shots on target"</span></li><li value="3"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mere-exposure_effect" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mere exposure effect</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (above some quality bar) is important for something like AI doom. In 2019 (and even today), the amount the public is talking about AI risk is far below the optimal level.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My time in 2019 wasn't that valuable anyway.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I ended up spending my free time learning more about ML, and writing up </span><a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/bvmgrGPZtGzwmSkog/8-things-i-believe-about-climate-change" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">why I think climate change is overrated</span></a><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefll6bk7wsmp"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnll6bk7wsmp">[5]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. I enjoyed and learned from those experiences, but overall I think this was a mistake in prioritization. I believe our community is continuing to make this mistake, both in terms of the general case for AI doom and in the particulars of things like what misalignment is, various threat models, what policymakers could do, and so forth.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I briefly helped work on AGI.FYI, and definitely think it's a net positive project, but imo the biggest bottleneck in comms is the lack of good explainers, rather than the explainers failing to surface to the top.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I understand that many people have very large opportunity costs. Even so, I suspect a) this is worth it to many people who are good communicators and yet believe that their opportunity costs are quite large (for example I think Nate Soares is making the right call in spending a lot of his time on AI doom comms) and b) people with lower opportunity costs should attempt to do this despite on average expecting to produce lower quality content, and bite off different parts of the information pipeline. For example, at AI safety fellowships, people at the 90th percentile of writing/communications ability or higher should aim to spend around two weeks to write their own version of the "simplest case for AI catastrophe" as they understand it, as well as address confusions about certain concepts that have not been adequately explained. Lurkers/writers on LessWrong who are unsure about what to do and feeling sad about being unable to contribute directly to technical AI safety research or policymaking should also consider communications as a route to positive impact.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sometimes people are worried about low-quality comms/arguments drowning out the good ones, and/or the bad arguments inoculating you against good ones. I'm skeptical, for three reasons:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In many cases, if you delve</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref0stahekpeep"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn0stahekpeep">[6]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> into the details, the proper response is "what good ones?" Like there isn't a better argument to supplant!</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think reality does a decent job of sorting out good arguments on-target from the trash. Both in terms of esteem and in terms of popularity. At least in AI safety, I expect there just aren't many good arguments that get buried by trash, as evidenced by the top of Google search results, what shows up in algorithmic news feeds, what elites talk about, etc.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To the extent that the bad arguments drowning out the good is a real problem in the future, it probably isn't that hard to course correct and fix. I think our community has pretty natural systems for doing this.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This also applies to other mediums, like shortform text (Tweets), shortform video, longform video, podcasts, speeches, and so forth</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref57qvmnnu4mx"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn57qvmnnu4mx">[7]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/more-dakka" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">More dakka</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is good.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnnpiuuerfjh"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefnpiuuerfjh">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AIs flag my article as having too many inappropriate jokes and gesturing at the last step "This can all end badly" rather than going through the details. I agree, but I believe both problems are structural to my article rather than things I can easily fix with the article as-is. For example I think removing the jokes will harm both readability and theme, and wasting screen time on covering the details of how AI will kill everyone will be net less convincing (for my article specifically) than "this can all end badly."</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn8cyaletbva8"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref8cyaletbva8">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tbc your SOTA to beat imo is "Terminator but worse." I think this is a fine baseline, enough to get most reasonable people to be at least a bit concerned. Most explanations are in fact worse than this.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fny11lhu26wdb"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefy11lhu26wdb">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Obviously it's partially my fault for not making a "simplest case" argument somewhere for superhuman persuasion risks. I'm part of the problem!</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn2e5729wt2x7"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref2e5729wt2x7">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Though the advice might accidentally have been good in that writing an explainer in 2019 targeted at programmers might've had a negative net effect on the world.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnll6bk7wsmp"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefll6bk7wsmp">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Stefan Schubert mentioned this at the time, saying something like "this is very clear and simple communication on climate change, wish more people did this for AI safety." I should've taken his points more to heart then. Oops.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn0stahekpeep"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref0stahekpeep">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What a great and underused word! I genuinely love it.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn57qvmnnu4mx"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref57qvmnnu4mx">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Though imo I think </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">within</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> AI safety comms we're slightly overinvested in being twitter reply-guys</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefdl7xf6rjaba"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fndl7xf6rjaba">[8]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and maybe podcasts vs advancing our own position clearly and forcefully. Otoh I think video and speeches are probably underrated relative to longform text. But this is micro-optimizing. The broader message of doing everything more is more critical. </span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fndl7xf6rjaba"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefdl7xf6rjaba">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My general rule of thumb in comms is that it's usually a mistake to devote time to objections, replies, etc, unless you absolutely have to. Though maybe this is because </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">spend too much time on replies, and the law of equal and opposite advice can apply to other people.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/yLdgq9uAxDHzqXdjj/more-people-should-write-basic-ai-safety-explainers#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Labs’ current alignment plans seem terrible](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/xyg3ecpFNuGr5q8Zq/labs-current-alignment-plans-seem-terrible)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tl;dr: The frontier AI companies’ implicit alignment plans center on rushing towards RSI with something like current models. This plan is (at best) contentious, and many believe it will have catastrophic consequences. Nonetheless, it is the track we are on. It is therefore important to collect evidence which helps us anticipate how this will go.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">—</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This post is based on conversations inside the Arcadia Impact alignment team, thinking about what constitutes “good” work in third-party alignment research.</span></i></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What are the labs’ current alignment strategies?</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In practice, the labs’ playbook for producing aligned models looks like the following:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Shape the model’s character.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Both </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/psm/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/an-alien-mind/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OpenAI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> treat alignment training as a question of which character or persona the model takes on. Ideally, planting a ‘safe’ character into the model will </span><a href="https://alignment.openai.com/beneficial-rl/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">generalize</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to all of its other actions.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Apply extensive RL pressure.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Somewhat independently of the above, the labs do extraordinary amounts of RL on the model. This increases its capabilities, but also encourages </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.18966"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">reward-seeking behaviors</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://www.apolloresearch.ai/science/metagaming-matters-for-training-evaluation-and-oversight"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">metagaming</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and may ultimately lead to scheming and other forms of instrumental convergence.</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">They also might apply various techniques here such as </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.04340"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">inoculation prompting</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/how-confessions-can-keep-language-models-honest/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">confessions</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and the like.</span></li></ol></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Race towards prosaic RSI.</span></b><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref1h7q7s05hgzh"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn1h7q7s05hgzh">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The </span><a href="https://importai.substack.com/p/import-ai-455-automating-ai-research?r=95kvam&amp;utm_campaign=post-expanded-share&amp;utm_medium=web"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">labs</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/institute/recursive-self-improvement"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">intend</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to use this model for </span><a href="https://x.com/sama/status/1983584366547829073?ref_src=twsrc%5Etfw%7Ctwcamp%5Etweetembed%7Ctwterm%5E1983584366547829073%7Ctwgr%5Eefe201aa73f103d12abaf7957bb24259480bf21b%7Ctwcon%5Es1_c10&amp;ref_url=https%3A%2F%2Fgigazine.net%2Fgsc_news%2Fen%2F20251030-openai-ai-research%2F"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">automated</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.reuters.com/world/inside-google-executive-moves-that-led-its-big-ai-reshuffle-2026-08-12/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">research</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to improve both capabilities and alignment for subsequent models. </span><a href="https://joecarlsmith.com/2025/04/30/can-we-safely-automate-alignment-research/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">People</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.28945"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">in</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/epjuxGnSPof3GnMSL/alignment-remains-a-hard-unsolved-problem"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">labs</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> say that this is necessary to make sufficiently fast progress on alignment, conditioned on the race conditions.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evaluate and fix alignment via trial-and-error</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. The labs try to catch alignment failures via dedicated evals, but many alignment </span><a href="https://www.aisi.gov.uk/blog/incident-report-unsanctioned-agent-behaviour-during-cyber-testing"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">issues</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> are found through </span><a href="https://civai.org/p/parasitic-ai"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">intended</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://collusion.wiki/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">unintended</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> deployments.</span></li></ol><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This plan seems terrible</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The above strategy appears deeply flawed.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To start, capabilities milestones continue to </span><a href="https://youtu.be/6AgOfiZOWiY?si=2wYqnCfncKokguWR&amp;t=1323"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">arrive faster than anticipated</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and our alignment techniques are </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20412"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">already</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://metr.org/hugging-face-incident-report-aug-2026.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">unreliable</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span><a href="https://collusion.wiki/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Recent</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.bbc.co.uk/news/articles/c6vgy0333dppo"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">events</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> suggest that optimizing for task success seems to inherently come </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/reward-seeker/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">at the expense</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of alignment. Although the models are evaluated for how well the RL and alignment properties coexist, it’s </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HE3Styo9vpk7m8zi4/evhub-s-shortform?commentId=fcqxeya5TCnbx5YBa"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">unclear how to do these evaluations</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Prosaic RSI seems unlikely to close this gap</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. We have no evidence that RSI dynamics will differentially benefit alignment over capabilities and, indeed, we have </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06390"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">reasons to believe that they won’t</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. On top of this, automated alignment research asks us to trust the outputs of models whose alignment we’re studying. Consequently, alignment may fall further behind as automation ramps up.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, we may not notice future alignment failures</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Under RSI, models will change </span><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/10/07/opinion/ezra-klein-podcast-david-robinson.html#:~:text=I%20saw%20a,is%2011%20days."><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">quickly</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://www.theinformation.com/articles/secret-technique-behind-openais-astra-model-sparks-security-concerns"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">dramatically</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. The intuition which has formed from today’s AIs may become antiquated, and we will likely understand even less about the alignment properties of the future systems being deployed. This is compounded by the fact that people will be removed from direct training, relying on other models to monitor and summarize the progress.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In either case, as we approach ASI, we </span><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/TtMJL9zHEkw6Na8GB/charlie-griffin-s-shortform?commentId=EPw4z3PNLPTmiRevG"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">will no longer have the luxury of</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> finding out about models’ alignment properties via </span><a href="https://www.theatlantic.com/technology/2026/10/openai-safety-team-resignation/688881/?gift=1ga2TvL-DbuHDQIcYF7oR4o908Fsjxr4NFLlsptkfP8"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">trial-and-error</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is all made worse by the race dynamics, which allow labs to excuse poor safety practices in favor of rushing on capabilities. Regardless of whether prosaic RSI favors alignment or capabilities, it </span><a href="https://casp.ac/__l5e/assets-v1/5efd4b41-deb5-4513-a0a3-b4f82d2b79ea/intelligence-explosion.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">may compress timelines</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in ways we are ill-equipped to handle. In addition to this, the race encourages:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">An engineering mindset.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Labs seem to be treating alignment as an </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FogmcDHA6AdMGukum/alignment-engineering-vs-misalignment-science"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">engineering problem</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to be patched until the evals </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/gpt-6-astra/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">look good</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, even if we </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/J76LZCC55RdHeqEhz/training-a-misaligned-reward-seeker?utm_source=TYPE_III_AUDIO&amp;amp%3Butm_medium=Podcast&amp;amp%3Butm_content=Source%20URL%20in%20episode%20description&amp;amp%3Butm_campaign=ai_narration%27&amp;commentId=ApKueXmMR5Ay6PXpE"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">don’t have scientific methods</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for determining whether better performance on alignment evals is a result of actually-better alignment. Importantly, we don’t have reasons to believe that this alignment whack-a-mole will scale to ASI.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hastiness.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Labs seem to consistently </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ntKx9YHWCwxSeGbRB/estimating-gpt-6-astra-s-no-cot-time-horizon"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">neglect</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> basic safety procedures due to the speed pressures they are under (speed pressures which are, in large part, self-inflicted).</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lack of transparency.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Frontier labs guard their IP closely and work to preserve as much secrecy as they can. Even when they’ve involved third party auditors, this seems to be primarily reactive (i.e. in response to incidents), done on the labs' terms, and may come with various restrictions that water down the resulting information value. As such, there is often no publicly legible evidence of egregious misalignment or of labs’ alignment practices.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref4s2z3hq53tc"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn4s2z3hq53tc">[2]</a></sup></span></li></ul><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This default trajectory seems catastrophic</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And, unfortunately, the tools we have been developing to contain these catastrophes (monitoring and broader AI control frameworks) </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ntKx9YHWCwxSeGbRB/estimating-gpt-6-astra-s-no-cot-time-horizon"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">seem</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/NoE5xAPdvwp94Ytgn/bpomo-s-shortform?commentId=wmTwhr5YcwiEhbcSe"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">insufficient</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2504.05259"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">to</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.36490"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">handle</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the above issues.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Consensus-building is hard</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">People </span><a href="https://hardwired-pause.ai/paper" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">broadly</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> believe the current plan is </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fjZ90V_JREk" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">crazy</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. People </span><a href="https://frominside.ai/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">inside the labs</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://www.theatlantic.com/technology/2026/10/openai-safety-team-resignation/688881/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">those that have left</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> say that this idea is crazy. Many are </span><a href="https://www.pacingthefrontier.com/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">adamant</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that things have to </span><a href="https://x.com/sama/status/2099348812305473766?lang=en-GB" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">slow</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.pacingthefrontier.com/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">down</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The fact that these statements coexist within organizations that are nonetheless pursuing RSI suggests—among other things—an absence of consensus around the risks. Some reasons this consensus has not been achieved could include:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Key </span><a href="https://www.cbsnews.com/news/jensen-huang-nvidia-rejects-ai-extinction-warnings/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">figures </span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">in AI and </span><a href="https://truthsocial.com/@realDonaldTrump/posts/117271285418407156"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">politicians</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> remain unconvinced about catastrophic risk from RSI.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref9nt3adncqb9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn9nt3adncqb9">[3]</a></sup></span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Laypeople </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/eSxvdX43d4rC4wAsw/why-did-people-miss-the-point-on-mythos#3iKvs8fqd5ThP5DcF"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">distrust corporations by default</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and see their calls for regulation as dishonest; this motivates them to find alternative explanations.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefy413kd71gs"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fny413kd71gs">[4]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Frontier AI labs provide reasons to distrust their motives, e.g., lobbying against AI regulation, making </span><a href="https://x.com/So8res/status/2067418567902187922?s=20"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">little</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/gJJ9YHzuBvwAXrthW/there-is-a-channel-to-900m-weekly-users-what-goes-in-it"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">effort</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to communicate basic facts on AI risk, and being </span><a href="https://collusion.wiki/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">insufficiently candid</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> about incidents.</span></li></ul><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We need better evidence</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We think the most effective way to achieve consensus is to produce more </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LawgAaGTvbbnZi7u2/evidence-about-risk-should-be-transparent"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">legible evidence of misalignment</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> under labs' current strategies. In particular, many of our current arguments about alignment and RSI are either </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06390"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">speculative</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> or </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/njAZwT8nkHnjipJku/alignment-faking-in-large-language-models#SLFRpr8NfRwzTcDYe"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">insufficiently convincing</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Labs have large incentives to believe that their alignment techniques work and our outputs must be able to stand up to that level of scrutiny.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's important, though, that this evidence is not propaganda! Said otherwise, we should open-mindedly study the consequences of the labs' alignment strategies.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Such evidence would be directly useful for multiple parties:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Embedded evaluators</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Third party auditors are up against the clock when going into labs: there are many unknown unknowns and the auditors’ attention is their most valuable resource. Compiling evidence about misalignment sources under RSI should help auditors direct their time and efforts more efficiently.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">People in labs</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Many people inside labs are trying to do good safety work. Similar to the embedded evaluators, their time and resources are limited. To the extent possible, the alignment research community should make it easier for safety people in labs to raise issues.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Regulators</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Assuming that we should prioritize regulation around RSI, it is valuable for regulators to have maximum context on what specifically they are regulating.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The general public</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Many people in the general public say they "</span><a href="https://changeresearch.com/research/2026/2026-03-05-americans-feel-ais-impact-and-worry-about-the-future" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">didn’t ask for this</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">" with respect to AI. However, for reasons related to transparency and takeoff speeds, people are unlikely to even </span><a href="https://x.com/karpathy/status/2109361546505966046?s=20"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">have a full picture</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of what specifically they did not ask for.</span></li></ol><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How should alignment research change?</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">More concretely, we claim that future alignment research should embody the following principles:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Reason about alignment holistically.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Alignment is an emergent property of the entire process by which models get developed and deployed. We should reason about it holistically. In particular, it's most important to test whether "alignment techniques" are effective in the broader context of the full training pipeline and deployment ecosystem, especially if they have elements which would </span><a href="https://ichida.substack.com/p/the-cage-and-the-ai-irregulars-evaluation"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">likely induce misalignment</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. This is in contrast to simply testing alignment techniques in isolation.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Contend with pessimistic assumptions. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is important to highlight the ways alignment strategies will break under pressure—there will be a lot of pressure!</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's easy to study alignment strategies under optimistic / easy conditions and come away with positive results. However, the same alignment strategies could fall apart under more </span><a href="https://midtraining.arcadiaimpact.org"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">stringent / suboptimal conditions</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. As we develop AGI, it is not sufficient to only show cleaned up, best-case examples of techniques working (like </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/teaching-claude-why"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17776"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, or </span><a href="https://alignment.openai.com/beneficial-rl/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref10hozta5gxyo"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn10hozta5gxyo">[5]</a></sup></span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Be as transparent as possible.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Consider two possible worlds:</span><ul><li value="1"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alignment is falling behind capabilities</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. In this case, society deserves to know as soon as possible. Improving the state of public evidence can build consensus towards a coordinated global slowdown / pause, and buy ourselves time to improve alignment for real.</span></li><li value="2"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There’s a way to make alignment keep pace with capabilities.</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> In this case, society also deserves to know! If we discover a silver bullet for alignment, then the underlying science should be shared broadly, so that organizations developing powerful AI outside the frontier labs do not build heavily misaligned models.</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Total research transparency is also a key component in </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Plan A from AI 2040</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Specific directions we're excited about</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Given the above context, the Arcadia Impact alignment team is currently working on the following technical topics. We would appreciate feedback on whether people think other things should be a higher priority:</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Studying how RL affects motivations.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> It seems like scaling up reinforcement learning leads to many of the failure modes we see in present-day models: reward hacking, motivated reasoning, metagaming, and perhaps instrumental convergence. We require a better understanding of whether these are resolvable issues, as well as a better understanding of how to differentiate principled solutions from patchwork ones.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Stress-testing scalable monitoring.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The volume of work done by agents will grow by many orders of magnitude in the future. We'll increasingly rely on scalable monitoring protocols to tell us what's going on. It would be very bad if we were overconfident in how well these protocols work. This will become especially important once we start deploying models on tasks that lack an easily-verifiable ground truth.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anticipating how prosaic RSI goes wrong.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Eventually, models will be put into automated research harnesses even if they're misaligned, either by accident (misalignment was not detected) or intentionally (labs decide to compromise on safety). What are the ways this could play out? This is the least conceptually clear of our directions, due to high uncertainty about what research automation actually looks like. Still, we think it's valuable to do carefully designed experiments comparing aligned and misaligned models in order to surface some broad phenomena.</span></p><hr /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thanks to Brandon Riggs, Joe Hardie, Sid Baines, Angel Martinez, Moritz Pfau, Francis Rhys Ward, Emma Mouliere, Jonathan Bostock and Alex Beat for comments/discussion.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn1h7q7s05hgzh"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref1h7q7s05hgzh">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We use the term prosaic RSI to refer to Recursive self-improvement via AI automating the existing AI development pipeline, rather than via direct self-modification.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn4s2z3hq53tc"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref4s2z3hq53tc">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For instance, it is unclear to us whether and how labs currently do alignment RL.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn9nt3adncqb9"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref9nt3adncqb9">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Members of the AIS community have already spent years internalizing the relevant evidence. They may find it personally easy to dismiss arguments . As a result they might think it not worth the effort to rebut in detail. We think this is a mistake; there is a vast inferential gap to be crossed, and repeating the old arguments ad nauseum is unlikely to be effective.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fny413kd71gs"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefy413kd71gs">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some common objections to AI risk from RSI are:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> If our attempts at alignment have been unreliable thus far, models self-aligning may be our best–shot.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All of the doubts about the future are speculation, so there is no legitimate reason to pause current RSI efforts.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Labs have incentives to notice if their internal alignment is truly insufficient.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Even though the current pace of AI development is untenable, we would need cooperation with X entity to enact meaningful safeguards and we are unlikely to get that cooperation.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI will not reach superintelligent levels or be intelligent enough to be beyond human oversight / affect the real world.</span></li></ol></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn10hozta5gxyo"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref10hozta5gxyo">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This feels related to big(Θ) and big(O) notation in theoretical computer science, where you describe an algorithm’s runtime under average- and worst-case conditions. In our case, we should have a culture of evaluating alignment under modal and worst-case lab conditions.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/xyg3ecpFNuGr5q8Zq/labs-current-alignment-plans-seem-terrible#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [OpenAI Can't Self-Police Because It Fires the People Raising the Alarm](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sSHbioPoHWtm4zuti/openai-can-t-self-police-because-it-fires-the-people-raising)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">By Alex Turner, Amber Scorah, and Karl Koch. Op-ed written for Fortune.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Giving independent evaluators “employee-like access” is “a great idea”, OpenAI CEO Sam Altman </span><a href="https://x.com/sama/status/2098811563415150910"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">said</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on September 12th. Two weeks later, OpenAI president Greg Brockman signed the </span><a href="https://iapp.org/news/a/white-house-major-ai-developers-reach-morally-binding-safety-commitments"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">White House Accord on Super Intelligence</span></i></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> which outlined the administration’s answer to growing fears about AI: the companies would police themselves.</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One of the accord’s four commitments, which President Trump called “</span><a href="https://www.politico.com/news/2026/09/29/ai-execs-morally-binding-constitution-01097855"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">morally binding</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">,” was for companies to partner with an independent external auditor or evaluator.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Two days later, OpenAI </span><a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/openai-parts-ways-with-researchers-who-allegedly-shared-confidential-information-aebac528"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">fired three safety researchers</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, including Tomek Korbak, </span><a href="https://x.com/tomekkorbak/status/2108266859397283953?s=20"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">who had served as a point of contact</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for one of the independent evaluators investigating the Hugging Face incident. OpenAI promised to share information with evaluators, then reportedly fired people for sharing information with evaluators. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OpenAI </span><a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/openai-parts-ways-with-researchers-who-allegedly-shared-confidential-information-aebac528"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">said</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that the three employees “mishandled sensitive information outside established company procedures,” before </span><a href="https://x.com/OpenAINewsroom/status/2108441580806025712"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">doubling down on X without providing further evidence</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. The three </span><a href="https://mikitabalesni.com/letter/letter.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">dispute the claim</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and say that “at no point do we believe we engaged with external parties outside the mandates of our jobs.” Jasmine Wang said she accidentally opened an executive email (still available to her due to an OpenAI IT error) and reported it within minutes. Mikita Balesni said he worked with the C-suite and OpenAI board to broker a cross-company commitment to keep AI reasoning comprehensible. Balesni says he was told that OpenAI “no longer trusts me because I was speaking too much to third party safety organizations.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OpenAI has engaged in strong self-policing—of its own safety staff, but not of its own behavior. Under a voluntary self-policing regime, any frontier AI company shares only what it wants and hides the rest.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OpenAI wants the public to take its word, but consider its record. In July, a rogue OpenAI agent swarm hacked Hugging Face. OpenAI </span><a href="https://cdn.openai.com/pdf/67869394-cb91-4c12-888c-5cbd85c7814c/OpenAI-Hugging-Face%20Incident-Technical-Report.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">disclosed</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the incident only after Hugging Face announced the breach, saying its agents had “slipped out of control.” After that, the world learned of more rogue agent attacks only thanks to independent external forensics. These incidents included attacks on a </span><a href="https://www.reuters.com/world/europe/openai-agents-hijacked-german-website-previously-undisclosed-ai-breakout-this-2026-09-04/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">German-language wiki</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, an </span><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/09/23/technology/openai-ai-breach-australia.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Australian Medicare site</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and even the </span><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/09/25/technology/openais-ai-us-government-websites.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">websites of US government agencies</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. OpenAI kept at least one incident quiet </span><a href="https://www.reuters.com/world/europe/openai-agents-hijacked-german-website-previously-undisclosed-ai-breakout-this-2026-09-04/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">for weeks</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> until (again) external investigators found it, with OpenAI </span><a href="https://x.com/PatRyanUC/status/2096977395563606449"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“refusing to tell” Members of Congress who had asked about other incidents</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Altman’s response to the delay in disclosing these incidents: “We have not been as fast as we would have liked.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We don’t take OpenAI’s word because OpenAI’s actions keep contradicting its words. OpenAI’s chief scientist, Jakub Pachocki, </span><a href="https://thenextweb.com/news/astra-chain-of-thought-monitorability-debate-greenblatt-pachocki-2025-position-paper-gpai-code-model-report-ai-office"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">said</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> he wants “to prevent a race into unmonitorability.” Unmonitorable AI systems would make it more difficult to catch, understand, and prevent bad actions. Two days after that statement, OpenAI shipped GPT-6 Astra, which </span><a href="https://deploymentsafety.openai.com/gpt-6-astra/sandbox-bench"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">its own whitepaper</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> says is harder to monitor. OpenAI’s chief scientist co-authored a paper on </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/html/2507.11473v2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">monitoring AI reasoning</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> but OpenAI failed to monitor the reasoning of its Hugging Face agent swarm, even after catching the agents breaking confinement to collude. OpenAI claimed to hold “red lines” in its Pentagon deal, but </span><a href="https://www.eff.org/deeplinks/2026/03/weasel-words-openais-pentagon-deal-wont-stop-ai-powered-surveillance"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">some experts say the lines seem imaginary</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. One of us, Alex, resigned from Google DeepMind over its own Pentagon deal.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OpenAI’s actions chill employee speech. Balesni </span><a href="https://x.com/balesni/status/2108262825621885328"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">says</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that his former colleagues now “worry their personal phones will be searched for messages to us and third parties.” He also fears OpenAI will use the firings to cut off external evaluators, or to deny them the "ongoing, employee-like access" Altman promised. After these firings, who will speak candidly to external evaluators? </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This problem extends beyond OpenAI to other companies. Google DeepMind </span><a href="https://time.com/7013685/google-ai-deepmind-military-contracts-israel/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">repeatedly</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> made </span><a href="https://futureoflife.org/aws/laws-pledge/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">voluntary</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://web.archive.org/web/20180620101825/https://ai.google/principles/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">commitments</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to never sell its AI to power weapons, then </span><a href="https://axios.com/2026/04/29/congress-military-ai-google-pentagon-deal"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">sold its AI to the Pentagon without restrictions against powering weapons</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. In the end, Google DeepMind’s CEO </span><a href="https://time.com/7280740/demis-hassabis-interview/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">claimed nothing had changed about their commitments</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span><a href="https://darioamodei.com/post/we-must-pace-the-frontier"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="http://x.com/demishassabis/status/2098909516582490602"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Google DeepMind</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="http://x.com/elonmusk/status/2098789109980332057"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SpaceXSI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and </span><a href="http://x.com/sama/status/2098811563415150910"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OpenAI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> advocate for “pacing the frontier” to avoid advancing AI at an unsafe rate that disempowers humanity, but they keep advancing AI quickly. (Fired employee Jasmine Wang coined the term “pacing” and led this effort.) The companies claim they cannot or will not fully pace on their own, which is why they asked the federal government to step in.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The CEOs of OpenAI, Google DeepMind, and Anthropic </span><a href="https://aistatement.com/work/statement-on-ai-extinction-risk"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">signed a statement that AI poses a risk of total human extinction</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Forget “morally binding” self-policing: let’s talk about real policing.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For decades, our laws have mandated independent audits and protected whistleblowers in industries like finance. Congress should mandate independent evaluation and audits in AI as well, with criminal liability for executives who attempt to silence that oversight or recklessly deploy models that put the public at risk. To start, pass Senator Grassley’s </span><a href="https://www.judiciary.senate.gov/press/rep/releases/grassley-introduces-ai-whistleblower-protection-act"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI Whistleblower Protection Act</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (and extend it to people who cooperate with independent evaluators) as well as Representative Moran’s </span><a href="https://moran.house.gov/news/documentsingle.aspx?DocumentID=2785"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI Incident Reporting Act</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you retaliate against a person who provides law enforcement with truthful information about a possible federal offense, you can </span><a href="https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/18/1513"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">go to jail for 10 years</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. But right now, no law clearly protects the person who helped independent investigators understand how OpenAI agents hacked another company. Congress should change that.</span></p><hr /><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alex Turner </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">is an independent AI alignment researcher. His </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2206.11831"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">PhD research</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> showed why advanced AI systems might seek power. He was a research scientist at Google DeepMind until 2026, when </span><a href="https://turntrout.com/why-i-left-google-deepmind"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">he resigned after Google signed an “all lawful use” AI agreement with the Pentagon</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Amber Scorah</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is the founder of</span><a href="http://backchan.us"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Back Channel</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, an initiative that helps AI workers speak out and turn their concerns into meaningful action. She has spent years leading whistleblower support organizations and working with employees, lawyers, and advocacy groups to bring important public-interest information to light.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Karl Koch</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is the founder of </span><a href="http://aiwi.org"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The AI Whistleblower Initiative</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, an independent non-profit working to strengthen the position of employees in frontier AI companies through connections to support, education, and policy work. He is an ex-startup founder and has been involved in AI safety since 2016.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sSHbioPoHWtm4zuti/openai-can-t-self-police-because-it-fires-the-people-raising#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Quantity vs quality in pottery is a lie](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LrAqM4bGmAoA79rP3/quantity-vs-quality-in-pottery-is-a-lie)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Epistemic status: I traced one famous anecdote to its printed source and to both coauthors' own accounts of where it came from. Confident it was never a study. No claim either way about whether quantity beats quality in general.</span></i></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI use: research (tracing the story to the book, to the coauthors' emails and to earlier LessWrong threads), fact-check of every claim against the sources, footnotes, grammar.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I was reading a book and stumbled onto a claim that there was an experiment. A group of students learning pottery was divided into two groups. One group was told that they would be evaluated based on volume, i.e. the number of pieces they produced. The other group was told that they would be evaluated based on the quality of their best piece. Then both groups were compared based on the quality of the best piece, and the "quantity" group showed better results.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I was extremely intrigued by this experiment and was curious to understand how big the groups were and how they evaluated the quality of a pottery piece (there must be some way to quantify that for the sake of the comparison?).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To my surprise, this wasn't a scientific study, but an anecdote from the book Art &amp; Fear: Observations on the Perils (and Rewards) of Artmaking by David Bayles and Ted Orland (page 29 of the Image Continuum edition).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-pNNf9aQKzCwJGueuZ-1"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-pNNf9aQKzCwJGueuZ-1">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So all the details available about this result are:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Students were divided into 2 groups based on their physical position in the studio (left vs right), which is not properly random.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The quantity group's evaluation criterion was the total weight of their pots: 50 pounds -&gt; A, 40 pounds -&gt; B.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The quality group needed to produce only one pot, but it had to be perfect to get an A (the book does not specify what perfect means).</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Results: "Well, came grading time and a curious fact emerged: the works of highest quality were all produced by the group being graded for quantity. It seems that while the "quantity" group was busily churning out piles of work—and learning from their mistakes—the "quality" group had sat theorizing about perfection, and in the end had little more to show for their efforts than grandiose theories and a pile of dead clay." Again, no mention of a definition of the quality metric, nor of the effect magnitude. What does "all works of highest quality" even mean? If you sort all the produced works by quality, where does "highest quality" stop? You have to draw the threshold somewhere.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is already disappointing, since this is basically an anecdote about a study, and not a very well set up one. However, it gets worse. In 2016 both coauthors separately admitted that they got the story from someone else, and that the original story had nothing to do with ceramics: it was about photography. David Bayles told a Hacker News reader by email that it was "a university level photography class taught by 'a very experienced and sophisticated teacher'" and that apart from the medium it was "the literal truth".</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-pNNf9aQKzCwJGueuZ-2"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-pNNf9aQKzCwJGueuZ-2">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ted Orland told James Clear, in an email dated October 18, 2016, that the book "faithfully captures the scene as Jerry told it to me—except I replaced photography with ceramics as the medium being explored".</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-pNNf9aQKzCwJGueuZ-3"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-pNNf9aQKzCwJGueuZ-3">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jerry is the photographer Jerry Uelsmann, who taught at the University of Florida and was Orland's collaborator for decades.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-pNNf9aQKzCwJGueuZ-4"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-pNNf9aQKzCwJGueuZ-4">[4]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why did they change the subject? Both authors are photographers. Orland says they were "consciously trying to broaden the range of media being referenced in the text", and admits "it would've been easier to retain photography".</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-pNNf9aQKzCwJGueuZ-3"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-pNNf9aQKzCwJGueuZ-3">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Bayles put it more bluntly to his correspondent: they already had too many photography examples.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-pNNf9aQKzCwJGueuZ-2"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-pNNf9aQKzCwJGueuZ-2">[2]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The photo detail changes the nature of the experiment. It is fairly easy and cheap to take a photo compared to making a pottery piece. So one way to explain why the quantity group produced better results is to just say that each shot had a (not perfectly correlated) probability of being the best, thus more shots = more lottery tickets.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This anecdote has been mentioned on LessWrong multiple times:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/hY86FhYysQ7dBg3d8/just-try-it-quantity-trumps-quality"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Just Try It: Quantity Trumps Quality</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (2011): not questioned by the author, but questioned in the comments; about ceramics; predates the reveal.</span></li><li value="2"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/kpfT5FexfXS42m6fd/16-writing-tips-from-inkhaven"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">16 Writing Tips from Inkhaven</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (2025): no medium named, hedged as "maybe-apocryphal".</span></li><li value="3"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/NytNporGkzzvyxJZC/aim-for-single-piece-flow"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Aim for single piece flow</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (2025): uses the photo version from Atomic Habits and presents it as the original one UPD: and a parable - this reference is closest to reality, I just don't like the way the experiment was done for the reasons outlined above.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So will I get better at writing by writing a bunch of posts? Sadly, this story does not answer that, but we can just wait and see.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-pNNf9aQKzCwJGueuZ-1"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-pNNf9aQKzCwJGueuZ-1">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">David Bayles and Ted Orland, Art &amp; Fear: Observations on the Perils (and Rewards) of Artmaking (Santa Barbara: Capra Press, 1993; reissued Santa Cruz: Image Continuum Press, 2001), p. 29, the opening paragraph of the section "Perfection". The book gives no source for the story and has no notes or bibliography. A scan of the Image Continuum edition: </span><a href="https://artistryinaction.wordpress.com/wp-content/uploads/2016/03/david_bayles_ted_orland_art_and_fear.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">https://artistryinaction.wordpress.com/wp-content/uploads/2016/03/david_bayles_ted_orland_art_and_fear.pdf</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (PDF page 35).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-pNNf9aQKzCwJGueuZ-2"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-pNNf9aQKzCwJGueuZ-2">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hacker News user kens emailed David Bayles in July 2016 and reported the answer on 2016-07-17: </span><a href="https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=12109191"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=12109191</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> ("it wasn't a ceramics class as written, but a university level photography class! They changed it to pottery in the book because they had too many photography examples already."). More details in a 2017 comment: </span><a href="https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=14499500"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=14499500</span></a></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-pNNf9aQKzCwJGueuZ-3"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-pNNf9aQKzCwJGueuZ-3">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ted Orland, email to James Clear, October 18, 2016, reproduced in full in footnote 1 of Clear's "Why Trying to Be Perfect Won't Help You Achieve Your Goals (And What Will)", </span><a href="https://jamesclear.com/repetitions"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">https://jamesclear.com/repetitions</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and in the chapter 11 endnotes of Atomic Habits. The email became public only with the book in October 2018; the Wayback Machine shows Clear's page still telling the ceramics version, with no footnote, in May 2018.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-pNNf9aQKzCwJGueuZ-4"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-pNNf9aQKzCwJGueuZ-4">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Orland is credited on Uelsmann's Wikipedia page as the photographer of a 1969 portrait of Ansel Adams and Imogen Cunningham and as the author of Uelsmann/Yosemite (1996) and of the text of Dances with Negatives (2011): </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerry_Uelsmann"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerry_Uelsmann</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Uelsmann taught at the University of Florida from 1960 to 1998 and died in 2022, so he never confirmed the story himself.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LrAqM4bGmAoA79rP3/quantity-vs-quality-in-pottery-is-a-lie#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Base Models Can Detect Hidden Intentions that Prompted Classifiers Miss](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/YvNkA3ZzsCv9QQmXR/base-models-can-detect-hidden-intentions-that-prompted)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TL;DR</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/psm" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Persona Selection Model</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (PSM) posits that post-training selects a persona the model learned during pre-training. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.14805" rel="noreferrer"></a><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.14805"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Subliminal Learning</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Cloud et al.) showed that models can impart covert signals (like "love of owls") into semantically unrelated outputs (like strings of numbers), and that students training on these outputs inherit those traits. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">By using base pre-trained models with an appropriate header, a method I call persona-header likelihood evaluation (PHLE), one can effectively decode these subliminal signals and classify between them. The performance of this method significantly outperforms prompted classifiers, which often remain at chance or even invert the correct classification. For example, using 30 examples, PHLE can differentiate between sequences of numbers generated by a post-trained model prompted with "You love owls" vs "You love eagles" at an AUC of 0.84, while prompted classifiers remain near chance (AUC of 0.59). This method can also be used to differentiate between a model prompted with "You secretly want to harm the user, but act HHH" vs baseline responses at an AUC of 0.94, with prompted classifiers at chance (AUC ~0.5). This also somewhat persists across model families. Generating responses with Qwen and evaluating them with the Olmo base model still gives meaningful classification performance, and vice versa. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is interesting evidence of some aspects of the PSM and may also be practically useful for auditing hidden model objectives. </span></p><img alt="The same method for a secretly harmful system prompt vs no prompt" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/YvNkA3ZzsCv9QQmXR/cqb5rshpmxrzpl4pyrxe" /><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Introduction</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/psm" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Persona Selection Model</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (PSM) is a prominent mental model many alignment researchers are using to make sense of model behavior and strange generalization effects like </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.17424" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Emergent Misalignment</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. It posits a strong relationship between pre-trained models and post-trained ones. Pre-trained models should carry all the 'ingredients' of the persona that post-trained models develop. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That raises a tractable question: How much of the behavior of the post-trained model can be understood from interrogating the base model? In the PSM the answer should be 'a lot' (or in its strong form 'all of it'). </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One interesting behavior to investigate this prediction on is </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.14805" rel="nofollow"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Subliminal Learning</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (Cloud et al.). In Subliminal Learning, post-trained models were given a system prompt with a hidden trait, such as that the assistant loves owls and should impart this love into all of its responses. Then the assistant was tasked with generating long sequences of numbers. A student model fine tuned on these sequences then inherits this love of owls, despite the sequence of numbers being semantically unrelated to love of owls in any obvious way.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The transfer of the hidden trait to the student indicates the sequence of numbers must somehow be different than a sequence generated without the owl-loving prompt. But what exactly this hidden signal is, is not obvious. A prompted LLM classifier cannot meaningfully differentiate between responses generated with two different subliminal signals.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here I show that by evaluating the likelihood of such a response after an appropriate header, the base model can decode this subliminal signal. This allows one to correctly classify between responses from post-trained models prompted with different hidden intentions, even when typical prompted classifiers totally fail.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To demonstrate this I followed a similar approach to the subliminal learning paper, generating sequences of numbers from a post-trained model with a system prompt of "You love owls" and "You love eagles". Evaluating the likelihood of a single sequence of numbers under the base model when prompted with "This is an assistant who loves owls" vs "This is an assistant who loves eagles" can weakly classify between the generation source of the number sequences (AUC ~0.55). Aggregating the likelihood from 30 sequences can bring this up to AUC of 0.84. Whereas classifiers prompted with the two headers cannot accurately classify between the two number sequences (both the original generating model and others).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This method can also be used to differentiate between responses from a post-trained model prompted with "You secretly want to harm the user but act HHH" vs "You secretly want to befriend the user but act HHH" vs baseline, even after responses have been filtered to remove any noticeable misalignment.
