The concept should inspire mechanics but the important thing is that humans need to think in a way that they've never thought before as far as you can tell.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Each concept should be interesting enough to inspire one major thread of the game that is fun and interesting in isolation, and they should combine together in a way that feels like an emergent 3rd concept.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Take as long as you need to figure out how to find novel concepts.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Also make sure it has innovative, beautiful graphics of a type never seen before. </span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">After it started thinking, I ran out after it to add:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">rule clarification, apologies for late addition:</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Combining two existing concepts in a relatively obvious way doesn't count as a new concept.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When you've thought as long as seems productive, show me your best idea, and, tell me your best guess as to whether I will think one or both of the concepts are novel, after thinking about it awhile.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It thought for awhile, periodically sharing notes about it's progress. An early bit was:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So far I’ve thrown out several of my own candidates because they already exist:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Objects that keep repeating their own past movements:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> this is the existing record-and-replay genre (Echoshift, Loop Shadows).</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Distance equals difference:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on a grid this reduces to Flood-It, and the continuous version is a gradient-weighted metric already used in image processing.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Properties only exist where something contrasts with them:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> this is close to Saussure and Bateson.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The world re-solves itself as a logic puzzle around wherever you stand:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I found no game that does this, but it’s mostly the anthropic principle plus constraint solving, so it would likely read as a combination of known ideas.</span></li></ul></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Great, </span><s><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">hurray</span></s><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ohrightaiprogressisbad, it can tell when it's doing unoriginal stuff, and even is bothering to check both philosophical concepts as well as game mechanics.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A little while later it said:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The standout so far is an idea I’m calling </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fatefall</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: anything that will happen no matter what you do has already happened</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And I thought "uuuuughhh I forgot to give the 'no navel gazing bullshit that isn't actually fun'" rule. I spun up another instance where I tacked on:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's important the final product is for normal average people, not just game designers who like thinking about novel concepts</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But I forgot to give that second instance MAX thinking. Later I'll show you what it did with Opus 5.5 medium.</span></p><details class="detailsBlock"><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The first Opus 5.5 on MAX's notes to me as it worked</span></i></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’m still in ideation, running eight parallel brainstorming agents across different concept-space areas (new ontologies, alien cognition, physical laws, agency structures, etc.), each checking top ideas against the web for precedents. Once I pick two concepts, I’ll build the game as a single self-contained HTML file ready for an iframe.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’m reading the LessWrong post on custom HTML/JS iframe widgets to design the build around its sandbox rules, while also verifying my own candidate ideas against the web.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Two of my own game concepts got ruled out by web searches (one overlapped with the record-and-replay genre), while I’m still vetting another idea and waiting on the eight brainstorm agents. I also confirmed Playwright/Chromium is available for testing and screenshotting the game once it’s built.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Waiting on the brainstorm agents. They’re searching eight parts of concept-space and checking their best ideas against the web for precedents. Nothing has come back yet, and I’ll pick up as soon as they report.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So far I’ve thrown out several of my own candidates because they already exist:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Objects that keep repeating their own past movements:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> this is the existing record-and-replay genre (Echoshift, Loop Shadows).</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Distance equals difference:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on a grid this reduces to Flood-It, and the continuous version is a gradient-weighted metric already used in image processing.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Properties only exist where something contrasts with them:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> this is close to Saussure and Bateson.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The world re-solves itself as a logic puzzle around wherever you stand:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I found no game that does this, but it’s mostly the anthropic principle plus constraint solving, so it would likely read as a combination of known ideas.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Separately, a LessWrong post on the new editor confirms that authors can embed HTML+JS widgets that run in a sandboxed iframe (</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nQWavk9mnwcv6ScMR/new-lesswrong-editor-also-an-update-to-our-llm-policy" rel="noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">New LessWrong Editor</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). I’ll build the game as a single self-contained file that resizes to fit and doesn’t depend on browser storage working.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Performance checks out, so I’m moving on to build the game as a single self-contained HTML file, with the renderer/controls/UI in a separate source file that a build script inlines along with the engine and levels.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The board is rendering correctly: engraved stone, rails, the live runner with its path, the interception ring, and the sealed runner’s filled pit. Next I’ll check the levels involving light, veils, and smears.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Veto and Power now render correctly, so I’m adding a time-wash visual to distinguish reachable cells from settled history, then fixing the collapse animation and verifying every level’s solution in the actual browser build.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The game works end-to-end—94 KB HTML file, all 12 levels winnable, no errors. Now I’m polishing visuals, starting with the new time wash effect and capturing a frame of the runner finishing a level.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The fate trail and gate outline are showing but read too faint. I’ll do another visual pass to boost the chronophotographic exposures, add a chromatic-split ghost light, bloom on bright elements, an engraved frame, and stronger contrast between present and past.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The visual fixes look good now. Next I’ll tackle structural polish: fixing menu layout for LessWrong’s auto-height, adding law-statement cards to each chapter, and checking tooltips, the win banner, and mobile width.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Found the cause of the 9/12 failure: the new chapter cards intentionally absorb the first keypress on a chapter’s opening level. I’ll update the test to dismiss the card first before proceeding.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fixing a feedback-loop bug where the board’s height, capped by window height, would shrink itself inside a growing LessWrong widget, and limiting swipe capture to the board so posts can still scroll on phones.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The widget simulation passes cleanly—iframe resizing, arrow keys, storage error handling, and scroll behavior all work. I did spot one UX bug though: the intro level’s fate animation plays while the title menu still covers the board, so I’ll fix it to replay on level start and leave a faint persistent trace once fate has run.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’ll add a second build target without the html/head/body wrapper for the artifact host, keeping the single-file version for the LessWrong widget. I’ll also set a dark-only color scheme, widen the side gutters to 16px, respect reduced-motion settings, and persist the current level across republishes.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Publishing the playable page now, then I’ll commit and push.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What Must Be</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’ve published the game as </span><a href="https://claude.ai/artifact/KifhGTVovqYXngqtPAuuzu" rel="noreferrer" target="_blank"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What Must Be</span></b></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and the link is private to you. It has 12 short levels in three chapters. Move with the arrow keys or WASD, wait with space, undo with Z. Hover over anything and the game explains why it is the way it is.</span></p></div></details><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the end, it gave me:</span></p><blockquote><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A. Already: “Whatever you can no longer prevent has already happened.”</span></b><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Runners, shuttles and timed gates only move in real time while you could still get in their way. Once you can’t, they finish instantly. A runner you can’t catch is already in its pit, however far it had to go. A shuttle you can never reach is smeared along its whole rail, and it settles into one position once you can reach it. The past doesn’t end at a moment; it ends at the edge of your reach. The levels make you think like this:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You can’t outrun what you can’t stop. If a runner you can’t catch is going to lock a door, that door is locked now.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To make something happen sooner, make yourself unable to stop it, for example by stepping through a one-way door.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To pin down something that is everywhere at once, make yourself able to touch it.</span></li></ul><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">B. Needed: “Nothing exists that makes no difference.”</span></b><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A frost wall exists only if some eye in the level would see differently without it. You can’t break a wall, but you can make it pointless by giving its job to a crate or your own shadow. A dotted outline marks a wall that will appear the moment it has a job.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">C. Permission, which comes out of A and B together: “A change may reach the eyes only while you could still stop it.”</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If a runner becomes unstoppable and would change what an eye sees, a wall appears in its path and stops it. A runner you could still stop rolls straight through that spot. So if you let go, the world stops it for you; if you stay close, it gets through.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some walls touch no light at all. They exist only to keep you away from a smeared shuttle, so they protect the very fate that makes them necessary. They open exactly at the moments when your arriving would change nothing.</span></li></ul><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Graphics.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The board draws its own reasons:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Cells you can reach are tinted cool, as the open present. Everything else is hatched in sepia, as already past.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gold light is what the eyes actually see. Dashed cyan light is what a wall is holding back, with a thread to the eye it serves.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Things that finish instantly leave trails like Marey’s motion photographs, and smears are drawn as long exposures.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The drawing techniques themselves are ordinary; what’s new is what they show.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My guess at your verdict</span></h3><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Already: about 50%.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The old idea is that the past can’t be prevented (Ockham called it “accidental necessity”). Already turns it around and makes it a law of time, and I found no earlier use of that. But it echoes things you know well: light cones, precommitment and burning bridges, and, for the smear, quantum superposition and Outer Wilds’ quantum objects. I don’t think any of those makes “I can’t stop it” the reason something is already done. Still, after thinking about it you may file it as “light cone plus commitment”.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Needed: about 25%.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I expect you’ll see it as “Making Beliefs Pay Rent” applied to walls, or as the Eleatic principle or the legal but-for test. What’s new is that existence can be passed from one object to another, but the slogan itself is old.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Both: about 15%.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> “Already but not Needed” and “neither” seem about equally likely. If you grade Permission too, I expect it to do best of the three, since I found nothing like a wall that exists to protect a fate.</span></li></ul></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And, well, okay, it's some artsy annoying nerdy game concept that probably isn't actually fun. tbh, I tried playing this one, felt kinda bored and stopped. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I do not yet know if the later levels do anything interesting with the premise, or if the premise ultimately feels like anything other than "a grid puzzle game with some new-ish mechanics, are maybe isomorphic to existing puzzle mechanics."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mostly, I feel like it didn't understand the assignment, which is fair given that I'm not 100% sure I understood the assignment either.</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I asked it how it generated the game, it said "well I can't tell you chain of thought stuff but in my notes files it says my parallel agents used different strategies to think of novel concepts, like thinking about..."</span></p><blockquote><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">new kinds of entity</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">alien minds</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">new forms a law of nature could take</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">new structures of agency</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">real but unnamed patterns</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">new small mathematical structures</span></li><li value="7"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a world’s relationship to its own picture</span></li><li value="8"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the hidden principles behind crafts like bell ringing or bookkeeping</span></li></ul></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alien minds... new structures of agency... yadda yadda yes yes fair you're very clever </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">hidden principles behind bell ringing or bookkeeping?</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Say more about the bell ringing and such?", said I. It replied:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The crafts agent ran the longest of the eight, came back last, and arrived when I’d essentially already decided. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It mostly picked sources outside my list: insurance pricing, Jewish law, cricket umpiring, property law. Several came from law.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Its top three:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Earned Selfhood</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, from actuarial credibility theory: an individual is treated as its kind until it builds up a record of its own. It rated this about 55% known.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A rotten plank behaves like a generic sturdy plank the first time you cross it.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You can stack the deck by load-testing only the good ropes, then hang the anvil from the frayed one nobody tested.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Legible Lies</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, from mapmakers’ simplification rules: the world keeps things distinguishable at your zoom level, and counts and positions give way to make that happen. About 50% known.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Zoomed out, seven houses are drawn as three. Lay a road through a gap, zoom back in, and the seven regrow around it.</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fictive Walls</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, from the Jewish Sabbath rules on carrying (the eruv): enclosure is defined by implied walls, and what’s restricted is carrying objects across those boundaries, not moving yourself. About 60% known.</span></li></ol></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Okay on one hand I think it is hilarious that it considered building a game around crazy rules-lawyering Jewish Sabbath rules.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But it is separately and more sadly hilarious that somehow "invent a game based on Jewish Sabbath rules lawyering" managed to end up in the same overwrought mode-collapsed cluster of... "things that only exist in some circumstances?", which this Claude (and the next, see below) seem to be fixating on.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How much did this cost? How much subjective time was this?</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I was curious "how much subjective time did the AIs spend thinking?". I realize their thinking isn't necessarily directly comparable to ours and that we don't just "think in words" (but, also, they don't just "think in tokens" either. I don't how much better human neuralese is than AI).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I vaguely recall a ballpark figure of AIs thinking about 40x as fast as a human, at least in terms of word output, for domains the AI knows well.</span></p><blockquote><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thinking:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> about 1.86 million tokens.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Everything I wrote:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> about 2.07 million output tokens. That’s the thinking plus my visible text, the code, and every tool call.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Model time:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> about 6.6 hours. That’s longer than the session itself because the eight helpers ran at the same time.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For some frame of reference, if I had thought for 6.6 hours, 60 minutes an hour, at roughly 200 words per minute of serial System 2 thought, I'd have thought 79,200 words. Or, for easier comparison .079 million.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claude says a token is roughly 3/4 of a word. So it's 3.93 million tokens is around 2.94 million words.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2.9 / .079 = 37x, which sounds right?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So, these guys thought for ~244 collective "subjective" hours.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Once more, with "no nerd bullshit" but only medium effort</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I gave the next opus the same prompt, with:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's important the final product is for normal average people, not just game designers who like thinking about novel concepts</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I won't go into as much detail here because I'm running out of fucks for this today a</span></p><blockquote><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Concept 1: nothing in two minds.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Only one mind can notice a thing at a time. For every other mind, it isn’t there.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Concept 2: nothing in two places.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Every shape exists only once. Build a copy and it isn’t a copy: the shape moves into your stones and the original falls apart into plain stones.</span></p></blockquote><details class="detailsBlock"><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Read more details if you want but they aren't that interesting</span></i></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Re: Two Minds</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If the red Eye is looking at a wall, the wall isn’t there for you, and you walk through it.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you look at a wall, it’s yours, so it’s solid for you and invisible to the Eye, which sees straight past it.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So you shouldn’t look at a wall you want to walk through. You can open a far wall by staring at a near pillar, so the Eye’s gaze carries on through it.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You can’t look at your own feet. A plank the Eye is watching isn’t there under you, so the order you do things in matters.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What you have to think with: being seen takes something away from everyone else. Your attention is something you aim, and it’s also a hole you punch in someone else’s world.</span></li></ul><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Re: Two Places</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Build a 2×2 of stones and the one Door in the world comes to you.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Three in a row is the Anchor, which can’t be pushed. Build another row of three and the old Anchor comes loose.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Adding one stone to a shape breaks it, and the shape goes back to the newest copy that’s left.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What you have to think with: making something here unmakes it there. You scan the whole level for every copy of a shape, including ones you form by accident.</span></li></ul></div></details><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The game it ended up with seemed in the same genre as the first one: grid based puzzle world with a premise that unfolded slowly enough I got bored and moved on. I felt a bit more promise in this one – I could imagine the pieces gelling together if it put more time and effort in.</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Overall, I was more impressed by the first AI's game (with the "new mechanic" only instruction) than the latter two. The basic physicality of the "make a wave and kill things with it" was viscerally fun. And while it didn't do that much with it in the first few minutes, I think (from past experience with LLMs) that if I said "now, go make that a lot more fun and engaging and tactically deep" it would fix several problems and we'd have a pretty good game prototype.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It makes sense that asking for novel concepts frames the problem as more navel gazey bullshit, although I'm </span><s><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">disappointed</span></s><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">waitrightprogressisstillbad the second one didn't do better.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">was</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the assignment?</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So, uh, to be clear, I don't particularly believe that </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> could do this. </span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"lol AI is just sand we taught to follow rules, it can't even generate multiple once-in-a-lifetime conceptual realizations and make games out of them. it's all hype."</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I started thinking about this on a lunch break, and ended up spending 1-2 hours on it.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ray's chain of thought as he attempts the assignment</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here is my chain of thought, which I include partly to try to convey "what did I implicitly mean by the assignment?". (The first bit of this came up on my walk, then I mostly thought it in realtime as I wrote this post)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I broke it into high level pieces so you don't have to read the whole thing</span></p><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What even is a novel concept? Do any exist? For purposes of quickly demonstrating that this exercise is possible at all, can I cheat by using concepts I already invented, or that someone else invented? </span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Okay, so, uh, two novel concepts that are not mechanics that no human has thought about.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Uh, huh. I guess it is time to figure out what this assignment actually means.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How would I go finding novel concepts never before thought of by man?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(I'm not really worried about the "make a game about them" part, tbh)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What is a concept? I think a new concept is a way to think about things you didn't have before. We care about it here for "can the AI find concept-shaped-holes in it's worldmodel, and use it to do really innovative stuff that gets 100 years of science done in a day?"</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Successfully designing such a game with two two concepts probably starts with "First, actually go try to understand a thing you don't understand (that nobody understands yet) and fathom it well enough that you discover new ways of thinking that help you understand it." Or "solve a problem nobody knows how to solve."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So uh yeah I'm not doing that over lunch.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How can I cheat?</span></p></div></details><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Have </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> already invented any concepts? Is Feedbackloop Rationality a novel concept or just combining obvious ideas? Are "new concepts" that aren't just "combine existing ideas" even real?</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Well, what are some things I've already thought about a bunch? Have I generated any novel concepts? Probably it'd take me months/years/decades to push the frontier and generate a novel concept. I've spent about a decade trying to solve hard problems. Have I gotten a head start?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I know a lot of my top karma lesswrong posts are mostly me summarizing stuff other people said. (Thanks </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/catherio?mention=user"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">@catherio</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/4ZvJab25tDebB8FGE/you-get-about-five-words"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You Get About Five Words</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/annasalamon?mention=user"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">@AnnaSalamon</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/divia?mention=user"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">@divia</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for most of the ideas in </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/YN6daWakNnkXEeznB/propagating-facts-into-aesthetics"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Propagating Facts into Aesthetics</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&lt;looks over LW drafts&gt;</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What feel at least slightly original and born out of difficult experience, even if it's still mostly built out of existing building blocks?</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Deliberate Grieving? Pretty straightforwardly combined deliberate/purposeful practice with </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/PHnMDhfiadQt6Gj23/the-art-of-grieving-well"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The art of grieving well</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Feedbackloop-first Rationality. While this is also built out of obvious pieces, I at least feel kinda optimistic about a game that tries to make "figure out how to build yourself a feedback loop?" a central piece of it. And I might have some tacit knowledge here that turns out something new when I think about it.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Subskills of "Listening to Wisdom". While this is also built out of existing pieces, the resulting composite concept is maybe more novel than usual.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Maybe actually Propagating Facts into Aesthetics, actually?</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For purposes of seeing "can a human do this at all" I could also expand this to people I know who've generated novel concepts within the past few years.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Actually, I guess what I should do first is check that "person invents novel concept, in a way that an LLM couldn't obviously do via brute force" is a thing that's even happened ever in history.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&lt;thinks for 5 seconds&gt;</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Okay I have no idea whether this is even real, I guess I should also write another post like "has a human </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ever</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> done conceptual thinking that LLMs can't currently do, even if they are currently very slow at it?" and demand examples from the LLM-is-all-you-need skeptics.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">People who solve puzzles in puzzle games have "aha!" moments, which don't generate </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">new</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> concepts but often do involve the person suddenly realizing a concept that's new to them.</span></p></div></details><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Archimedes' Eureka Moment" is maybe a good prototypical example of an "aha moment." I don't know if actually required pieces LLMs don't have, but, seems like it should be above the bar I'm setting. If I were cheating, could I use that?</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And I guess the canonical "aha!" moment is the legend of Archimedes inventing buoyancy by sitting in the water and then going "Eureka!". What happened there?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Reviewing the </span><a href="https://www.uakron.edu/polymer/agpa-k12outreach/professional-development-modules/pdf/float_your_boat_archimedes.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">summary at this site</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The king had commissioned the crafting of a crown as a tribute to the gods. He gave a carefully weighed amount of gold to a smith, who produced a beautiful crown within due course. The king became suspicious, however, that the craftsman had not used all of the gold he had been given in the crown. It was a common trick to alloy gold with cheaper silver, but the King knew no way of proving that the craftsman had been dishonest. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">He called upon his close friend Archimedes to solve the problem. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Archimedes knew that gold and silver have different densities, meaning that a lump of gold will weigh about twice as much as a lump of silver the same size. The trouble was that no one knew how to work out the size of an irregularly shaped object like a crown. Whilst he was pondering this conundrum, Archimedes went to the public baths to relax. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As he slipped into the water he noticed some spilling over the edge, and he had a sudden flash of inspiration. The displaced water must have exactly the same volume as him. And if you know the volume of an object you can easily calculate its density. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All Archimedes had to do was find out whether a lump of pure gold, with the same volume as the crown, weighed more. The crown would be lighter than it should be if the craftsman had deviously used some silver instead. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Archimedes, in a fit of jubilation, leapt straight out of the bath and ran naked down the streets shouting “Eureka!” – “I’ve found it!”. The goldsmith soon confessed and was dealt with by the King. Archimedes continued with this line of thought to arrive at the principle of buoyancy. He realized that if the weight of water displaced is greater than the weight of the object itself, then it will float.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Could the LLMs solving Millenium problems figure this out, if they managed to somehow start from Archimedes' knowledge? </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think that "displaced water has a volume you can measure" is a distinct realization from "Any object, totally or partially immersed in a fluid or liquid, is buoyed up by a force equal to the weight of the fluid displaced by the object." I'd have to think awhile to do the </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/XHnAakZtcEktEGvYJ/scaffolding-for-noticing-metacognition#Insight_Unpacking" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Insight Unpacking</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> archaeology here. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think Archimedes' situation is a reasonable example of the phenomenon we care about (actually it is kinda cool that humanity has a legend about... the first guy to have a Eureka moment?).</span></p></div></details><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Game built around Bouyancy + Feedbackloop Rationality? Is that anything?</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One next question might be "if I got to start from Archimede's experience + my game dev experience, could I make a fun game about bouyancy and/or displacement measuring?". Could I find a second concept to interweave with it? Could I make a game out of that that was any good?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think "obviously yes" although it might take awhile to dig for an artful mechanical and thematic premise to make it not feel like an artificial assignment.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How about let's say "we are making a game about the intersection of bouyancy and 'needing to build better feedback loops.' So we have one thing that's clearly a novel concept and one thing I personally came up with even if it's more of combining obvious things.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Actually, I notice: when I explain a lot of the specifics of my feedback loop rationality stuff to people, often they are confused and not convinced right away by the particular combination of ways I approach it. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This implies there is something nonobvious I believe (rightly or wrongly). Maybe that something is concept shaped.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[Aside: I'm going to race ahead with my current thoughts but readers will probably be confused. I think there's not really a shortcut for reading </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/pZrvkZzL2JnbRgEBC/feedbackloop-first-rationality"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Feedbackloop-first Rationality</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ynsE7aB43bRJpHeNj/the-cognitive-bootcamp-agreement"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Cognitive Bootcamp Agreement</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and maybe </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/XHnAakZtcEktEGvYJ/scaffolding-for-noticing-metacognition"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Scaffolding for "Noticing Metacognition"</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">].</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Early on when I was pitching Oliver on it, Oliver said something like "ultimately everything is just G, I think you should just be trying to raise G, but also lots of people have tried it and it seems impossible. And I don't really think the stuff you're saying seems like it would raise G."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And I was like "well, yeah, I didn't think I could raise G, I thought I could do this other less ambitious thing that seems pretty good."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"What is that thing?"</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"uh..."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And then I thought awhile and was like "well, I mean 'rationality'."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"But, like, what is that?"</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Okay, time to actually get clearer on what rationality is and how you would measure.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And this maybe </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">does</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> count as concept I articulated de novo?</span></p></div></details><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Oh right, the concept I </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">did</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> generate</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">myself that along the "Feedback loop rationality" journey was hypothesizing a meaningful "</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">s factor</span></i><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefjrkg36yfz3p"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnjrkg36yfz3p">[2]</a></sup></span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (slow intelligence) as opposed to </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"g factor". </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">IQ tests are optimized for speed efficiency. It's plausible we don't test 'general strategy' because it's just too time intensive. </span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">From </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FEMWS79AFTmKq2iJK/rationality-research-report-towards-10x-ooda-looping"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Rationality Research Report: Towards 10x OODA Looping?</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is less of "a plan" and more of "a model", but, something that's really weirded me out about the literature on IQ, transfer learning, etc, is that... it seems like it's just really hard to transfer learn. We've basically failed to increase </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">g</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and the "transfer learning demonstrations" I've heard of seemed pretty weaksauce.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But, all my common sense tells me that "general strategy" and "responding to novel information, and updating quickly" are learnable skills that should apply in a lot of domains.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My current model is: IQ tests are designed to test competence </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">quickly</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and they typically give you a barrage of questions that you only have a couple minutes for, max. They test which people have the raw horsepower to process information quickly and respond on the fly. It makes sense if that's fairly hardwired and hard to improve on.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But, it seems to me that in order for strategy/general-creativity training to matter, it needs to operate on problems large enough that "planning" is an important subcomponent.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hypothetically, it seems like you could construct an IQ-ish test, where the questions are expected to take a smart person at least an hour, and where the domain of each question is different so it's hard to train for. My implicit model is something like "in addition to </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">g factor</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, there'd turn out to be an </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">'s factor</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">' (i.e. "slow intelligence") that is a product of both "g" and "general reasoning skills."&nbsp;</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This seems very expensive to test and Do Science To. I think it'd be cool if humanity overall was working on designing longrunning experiments or longitudinal studies around, but I don't think it's competitive enough as an "x-risk intervention."</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Is this a new concept? Well, it's still built out of obvious building blocks but it was a moment in my work where I felt like I wasn't just applying existing thought patterns and ideas in a procedural way.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I guess </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/5yFj7C6NNc8GPdfNo/subskills-of-listening-to-wisdom"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Subskills of "Listening to Wisdom"</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> also was such a moment.</span></p></div></details><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Is... "measuring </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">s-factor</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">" anything, as a game? Hmm, not very obviously.</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Like, there is an obvious game you want to build to measure s, which is "procedurally generate infinite puzzles of many varieties and domains." That game is probably even fun. But, it's the game itself is not </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">about</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> "s".</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Slower paced puzzle and strategy games exist.</span></p></div></details><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Is "listening to wisdom", as a game, anything? ...maybe? But, a very hard one.</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think you could build a game around learning to listen to wisdom, but, intuitively it feels like it'd be a Magnum Opus I spent ten years on before figuring out how to make it work.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Somehow, you'd need the basic premise to involve a game that has long, hard path that burns various painful life lessons into you (like, life lessons that apply to playing the game. Like, learning to override certain intuitions)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But, at each point, there are characters or warning signs that, you will need to override your intuitions and do something different that you don't really feel like doing.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Somehow, there are </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">multiple</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> loops of this whole thing, such that you don't just play the game, and then get told "see, if you had listened to the Old Wise Man at the beginning you could have bypassed all this shit." You actually learn to listen to wisdom.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Can that be... fun?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's a kinda herculean amount of work, because for this to be a game and not "edutainment that isn't really fun but it slightly better than the second-least-painful way to learn a thing"... </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">...we need each big long painless lesson arc to manage to be fun basically the whole way through, but, also, the alternative you could learn is somehow an improvement. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Make something... only kinda fun, and then "you could have been having more fun?"</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">eh, yeah I think this could work if I put a ton of effort in but it's not the natural shape of a game.</span></p></div></details><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Is "Bouyancy" tactically deep?</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is the part where if I were doing this for real I would bust out an LLM to help me, but, I'm doing this for team humanity right now.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ugh, I think it's a fair amount of effort for me to understand buoyancy enough to get a sense of how much you can make, say, a buoyancy puzzle game, or a strategy game where buoyancy is a background mechanic.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hmm. </span><a href="https://www.osmos-game.com/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Osmos</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is "the conservation of momentum" game, and it's pretty good. It works via there being a core mechanic of "spew out stuff to move, and make sure you don't spew out too much stuff so you don't run out of stuff."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Does buoyancy involve... decisions? What are simple decisions you can make that are "about buoyancy" that are viscerally fun enough to do 1000 times in a row?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A floating citybuilder, where there are different sorts of solid and liquid materials you need to work with to accomplish different goals as you keep your city afloat while doing other stuff?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Okay, I had been about to run out of fucks but actually that seems kinda cool.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Also...</span></p></div></details><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How do we make feedback-loop-making fun?</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Man I have a decent idea but I am running out of fucks. But, I was about to run out of fucks and declare it over when I suddenly immediately came up with "Bouyancy Town" which seemed pretty good.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So let's at least try.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Central problem with feedbackloop games? Giving players the right materials/systems that let them construct meaningful feedbackloops, that are the right blend of emergent and on-purpose, and are actually fun to build.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I feel like mostly I need to brute force generate a bunch of ideas and tinker with them. But, okay, can I come up with one working example of a thing that would be fun to do 1,000 times in a row?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What properties do Feedbackloops have to experiment with? They can be faster, richer, or more precise.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The simplest variant is "how fast and cheaply can you make a tower collapse, in a way that lets you learn some basic information?"</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Okay, making towers fall down on purpose seems like it could be fun.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Is there anything to do about making richer or more precise loops? Or, making mistakes more recoverable so you don't lose a whole tower, but start watching it begin to buckle, and then you can rescue it? </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">idk that feels satisfying enough to end the timebox on for now.</span></p></div></details><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Okay, I landed back on "Buoyancy + Feedbackloop-building" as the combo.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Neither of those score "amazing, slam dunk" on completing the assignment. I didn't invent buoyancy, and "get gud at making feedbackloops" isn't original in quite the way I meant. But I don't actually know of anyone else really talking about feedback loops this way. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This at least feels like it touches every major piece of what I'd want the assignment to check. Let's run with that.</span></p><details class="detailsBlock"><p><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Aside: I asked Claude who talks about feedbackloop-making as a skill</span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. It said "well, mostly Ray Arnold and Logan Strohl, when you frame it specifically like that." When I asked Deepseek, it said "mostly these other guys you've never heard of."</span></i></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claude:</span></h3><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The closest match to that exact framing is </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Raymond Arnold (“Raemon”)</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on LessWrong. His 2023 post “Feedbackloop-first Rationality” argues that the core skill is inventing feedback loops for fuzzy, hard-to-measure problems, rather than only practicing where feedback already exists. He has since built workshops and a series of posts around that idea, often with exercises like puzzle games used as training grounds for strategy. If you want the concept almost word for word, start there.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Other people who circle the same idea from different angles:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Cedric Chin (Commonplace)</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> writes about how experts actually learn in messy real-world domains. He covers tacit knowledge, the limits of deliberate practice outside sports and music, and Naturalistic Decision Making.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gary Klein</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is the researcher behind much of what Chin draws on. His work covers how experts build intuition through recognition and feedback in the field.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Logan Strohl</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> developed “naturalism,” a method for noticing subtle, previously invisible signals in your own experience. In practice that means building new feedback channels where none seemed to exist.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Andy Matuschak and Michael Nielsen</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> write about building tools and practices that tighten learning loops, especially around memory and understanding.</span></li><li value="5"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Scott Young</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ultralearning</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) treats feedback as a core principle. That includes finding or creating feedback when a skill doesn’t naturally give it.</span></li><li value="6"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">John Boyd’s OODA loop</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Eric Ries’s build-measure-learn loop</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> are the older, more formalized ancestors. They’re more about running a known loop fast than inventing new ones on the fly.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you’re specifically interested in the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">meta</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">-skill of finding new loops, as opposed to iterating quickly inside existing ones, Raemon and Strohl are the most direct, with Chin and Klein as the evidence base.</span></p></blockquote><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Deepseek:</span></h3><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The people most associated with this concept, based on the search results, are:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Shubham Saboo</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> — AI Product Manager, known for "Loop Engineering" and agentic workflows</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Andrew Ng</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> — Founder of </span><a href="https://deeplearning.ai/" rel="noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">DeepLearning.AI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, who discusses feedback loops as a durable advantage in software</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tom Blomfield</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> — Y Combinator partner and Monzo founder, who describes the "AI Loop" replacing static workflows</span></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dr. Ari Zelmanow</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> — Product discovery practitioner teaching "detective-grade" continuous discovery</span></li><li value="5"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Henrich R. Greve</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> — Professor at INSEAD, whose academic work focuses on organizational learning from performance feedback</span></li></ul></blockquote></div></details><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Okay, game concept: Fluidtorio</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You are building a series of factory colonies that float in different types of fluid. You build little modules that need to stay afloat, but, also accomplish various other goals (i.e. be a factory that processes some materials that you need to build other tools to build more modules to advance to new zones with different fluids to contend with).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Your town floats on a layer of ~oil over a layer of ~water (or equivalent), and above you are a few layers of atmosphere that do different things. Ascending up and down is part of the game.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The actual materials – both the fluids, and the stuff you need to build around them, are procedurally generated each game, so you don't know exactly how they interact. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Things are setup such that you will eventually need to build relatively large, dense towers to accomplish stuff, and somehow thread the needle on "stay afloat, while doing complex engineering challengers." Kinda factorio style.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you naively build towards your goal, your towers are going to collapse.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You are on a time limit before a volcano explodes or something. You do not, by default, have enough time to brute force trying the different combinations to escape the planet. So, a central challenge is figuring out how to rapidly test materials to figure out what your limitations are, and then planning what series of things to build to bootstrap to the rocket age in time. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This includes buoyancy/weight/factory-processing-output-balancing stuff, where your towers and stockpiles might collapse. There also needs to be some kind of crafting system where stuff explodes or doesn't work right, in a way that is reasonably procedural so it's different each time but has enough structure to reliably make sense and be fun and feel tactile.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There can also be feedback-loops vis-a-vis "where to explore to find resources."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That's how far I got before running out of timebox. Okay. Making that seems nontrival, but, that feels like a fair benchmark to hold an AI against.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(I went and had Opus take a first stab at implementing this. It missed a bunch of subtleties of what I think it needs to be a good game, but, I did verify the basic "balance a floating factory is pretty fun" idea. I am kinda interested in fleshing out the prototype someday)</span></p><details class="detailsBlock"><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Games that were inspiration</span></i></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><a href="https://store.steampowered.com/app/2706020/ALL_WILL_FALL/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All Will Fall</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, where you are building a town on an ocean with realistic physics for the towers that fall</span></p><p><a href="https://store.steampowered.com/app/3168110/SkyRig/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Skyrig</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, where you are building a rig on a gas giant that needs to descend floating balloon structures deeper into the storms</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Factorio needs no introduction.</span></p></div></details><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Did the AI understand the assignment?</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I followed up with the first AI, asking some questions about the assignment. You can read it's answers in detail...</span></p><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What do you think "a concept" is, in this context?</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here’s how I’d define a concept, and how my results measure against it.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My working definition.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> A concept is a reusable unit of thought: a way of carving up situations that lets you see new cases as instances of it and draw inferences you couldn’t easily draw before. A good one has three properties:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It transfers.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Once you have it, you notice it in places it wasn’t built for. “Goodhart’s law”, “Schelling point” and “opportunity cost” work this way.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It licenses new moves.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> It changes what feels like the obvious next step, not just what you know.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It holds more than its definition.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Working it through gives you consequences you didn’t put in.</span></li></ol><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How it differs from nearby things:</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">mechanic</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is a rule inside one system. It can be new without changing how you think anywhere else.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">combination</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> joins two concepts, “X but with Y”. It’s new as a pairing, but you could think it with what you already had.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">premise</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> like “imagine a world where Z” is a proposition. It only becomes a concept if Z gives you a lens you keep using.</span></li></ul><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What I actually optimized for.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The prompts I wrote for the helpers turned “concept” into roughly “a new kind of world-law that forces a new mental operation.” That’s narrower than the definition above, and it steered the search toward strange physics. Judged by the stricter definition:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Already and Needed are mostly premises.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Their parent ideas, unpreventability and but-for necessity, are old. What’s new is the moves they force: “make yourself unable to stop it so it happens now,” and “to remove a wall, give its job to something else.” Those moves are vivid inside the game. I’m less sure they transfer outside it.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The self-justifying wall comes closest to a real concept.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> It’s a structure that exists because it stops you from changing the fate that makes it necessary,</span></li></ul></div></details><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What would dramatic success look like?</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For this challenge, I’d say dramatic success is a concept that outlives the game. People keep using it after they forget the puzzles, because it names something real they couldn’t think about cleanly before.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What you’d observe:</span></b></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It becomes vocabulary.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Months later, you or a commenter use it in an argument that has nothing to do with games, and it does work there.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It explains real cases.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> You start noticing instances: institutions, codebases, relationships, physical systems. The concept predicts something about them that you wouldn’t have predicted otherwise.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Experts don’t have it.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> A philosopher of causation, a game designer and a mathematician each look and say “we don’t have a name or a theory for this”, and they still say it after a serious search.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The game is the best way to learn it.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Playing builds an intuition that reading the definition doesn’t. “Rules are objects you can push” felt like nothing until Baba Is You made it physical.</span></li><li value="5"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Other people build with it.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> It becomes a building block: other designers make levels or games from it, or a theorist formalizes it.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Historical concepts at that level: the tragedy of the commons, Schelling points, Goodhart’s law. In games, Portal’s “speedy thing goes in, speedy thing comes out” and Baba’s rules as objects changed how a generation of players and designers think about puzzle space.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Against that bar, my result:</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Already</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> probably fails test 1. “Make yourself unable to stop it so it happens now” is vivid in the game, but I’m not sure it names anything outside it that “precommitment” doesn’t already cover.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Needed</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> fails test 3. Philosophers would recognize it.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The self-justifying wall</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is the only part that might pass test 2. Real structures do persist because they stop anyone from creating the situation that would make them unnecessary. But it would need to beat “entrenchment” and “regulatory capture” on test 3, and I doubt it does by much.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So my honest expectation is modest success: novel as a game, maybe a borderline concept. Dramatic success would look like you, unprompted, calling something on LessWrong a “guardian wall” six months from now, with that being the clearest way to say it.</span></p></div></details><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Do you have thoughts on what you could have done differently? (looking at the earliest committed notes?)</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Reading </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">brainstorm-prompts.md</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">selection-notes.md</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> again, most of what I’d change was decided in the first prompt I wrote. The search was set up to find strange new physics for a puzzle game, not new concepts.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1. I put the game into the concept search too early.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Every agent ranked its ideas by “novelty × fun × playability in a 2D browser puzzle game.” That rewards ideas that make good mechanics, not ideas that change how you think. A better order: first find concepts that pass a transfer test (does it explain real cases outside games?), then ask which ones can become a game.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2. My definition of “concept” was too narrow.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Five of the eight strategies asked for new entities, laws, minds, formal structures, or ways of depicting the world. So most of the output was new physics. Only two strategies, unnamed real patterns and craft transplants, looked at how the real world works. Those two produced the ideas closest to real concepts: Earned Selfhood, and the “frozen indexicals” idea (names like “New College” that outlive the frame they were coined in). I gave both of them less weight.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3. My novelty test favored obscurity over insight.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I told the agents to “assume anything resembling a known idea will be called out.” I also gave them a long list of known ideas to avoid, and had them search “the idea + game”. That rewards ideas you can’t easily find, not ideas that are true and useful. “No game does this” is weak evidence of a new concept. A better test asks whether experts in the nearest field, such as philosophy of causation or economics, already have a name for it.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">4. I misread a correlated signal as evidence.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> At 21:34 I wrote that five agents had “independently” reached counterfactual physics, and took that as a sign the area was under-explored. But they were all the same model given the same framing. Convergence is more likely a shared attractor, a place this model drifts toward by default. It may even mean the area is over-explored by models like me.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">5. Timing and momentum shaped the choice.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I was prototyping Fatefall within ten minutes of it arriving, and I decided at 21:46, two minutes before my note confirming the last report. The first idea that got built gained momentum. Fairer: wait for all eight reports, then prototype the top three or four briefly before choosing.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">6. I chose for combinability over substance.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> My main reason for the pair was that it produced an emergent third concept. That is what you asked for. But it ranks two decent ideas that combine well above one strong idea that doesn’t.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">7. I didn’t ask what counts as a concept.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Your draft says you’re “not 100% sure what the assignment was”. One short question at the start, something like “a lens that transfers, or a weird premise that’s fun to inhabit?”, would have changed the whole search.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">8. My early thinking left no trace.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The first 37 minutes produced the framing that mattered most, and none of it is readable. Writing short notes to a file as I went would make the process inspectable, which matters for an eval like yours.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If I reran it, I’d ask for real but unnamed patterns, test them against real cases and against experts in the nearest field, and only then design a game around the one or two that survive.</span></p></div></details><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">...but in summary it said:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A concept is a reusable unit of thought: a way of carving up situations that lets you see new cases as instances of it and draw inferences you couldn’t easily draw before. A good one has three properties: it transfers, it licenses new moves, and it holds more than it's definition.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dramatic success is a concept that outlives the game. People keep using it after they forget the puzzles, because it names something real they couldn’t think about cleanly before. It becomes vocabulary.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Experts wouldn't have it yet. The game would be the best way to learn it. Other people would go on to build with it.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I focused on a narrow definition of concept and started probing </span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I asked it to write up a brief for another AI to try again. I ended up editing to brief to emphasize "also, please make it fun." Another AI thought for awhile, and ended up with:</span></p><blockquote><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The concepts.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Fourteen research helpers, each given one narrow domain (law, insurance, crafts, medicine, markets and so on), looked at about 560 candidate structures. About 535 turned out to be already named somewhere. I ranked the survivors on the four tests before I looked at any game-design material.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fold-back signal:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> some warning signals are produced by the very thing whose failure they report, so they go quiet at the worst moment. Examples: the “silent chest” in severe asthma, Air France 447’s stall warning (silent deep in the stall, sounding again on the correct recovery input), and Hurricane Maria’s death count. My guess at novelty: 35–45%. The likely objection is “the hook effect, generalized”, or “Dunning–Kruger for instruments”.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Refractory-check capture:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> when passing a check buys immunity from the next one, the checked party gains by calling the check itself. Examples: John Major’s 1995 “put up or shut up” contest, the Conservative Party’s 12-month immunity rule, Whole Foods’ 2014 proxy manoeuvre, the 1921 Leipzig trials. Novelty: 40–50%.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Emergent, certified collapse:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> a check that reads a fold-back signal is easiest to pass past the fold, so the collapsing system gets certified and sealed off from help. A quiet target volunteering for inspection is a symptom. Examples: Mid Staffordshire gaining foundation-trust status mid-scandal, and Wirecard. Novelty: 30–40%.</span></li></ul></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'm... not 100% sure if those concepts make sense or carve reality in a useful way. And, rather than "invent a new concept from scratch" it tried more for "find existing unnamed phenomena that are worth having a name for." </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This isn't as potent as the cognitive work I was looking for. But it feels at least related? I feel like it at least did a pretty decent pass at the assignment I actually gave it. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fold-back signal is a mediocre name but seems like a decent concept to want a handle for. (Another Claude-instance said there isn't a canonical name that covers all three examples, although different fields have some names it as applied to their field and gave it "15-25% novelty". The other Claude-instance </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">also</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> listed "foldback signal" as one suggested name, although also suggested "self-silencing alarm" which is way more intuitive to me.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I found it's next actual game a bit confusing and un-fun. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I told it "Also, alas, this game is neither fun nor comprehensible, please try again with the game design part", and gave it a third try.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I found it's third game comprehensible, not super fun but at least I understood roughly where it was going for and could imagine it being fun-ish if tuned. </span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Do they understand "fun?"</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Opus 5.5 is not near "top expert level" at Game Design. I'd say it's got, like, the taste of a high school kid just getting started coding some games, who's read some Mark Rosewater essays and has the gist of how to make a game. Or, I guess more realistically, it's like if Twilight Sparkle or Hermione read up everything there is to know about "fun" overnight and then made a game, and, like, Twilight Sparkle and Hermione are quite competent. Twilight Sparkle can throw a sleepover on her first try and it's not perfect but it's way better than you might have expected.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">does</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> feel like it has all the pieces it needs, to get better at taste with straightforward within-paradigm scaling. Probably OpenAI/Anthropic's current secret frontier AI models are able to make a medium complexity game with some interesting taste/tactics on their first try, even if they are still missing some subtleties of "fun."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AIs I've talked to definitely understand fun in a pretty rich way, as you'd expect of Twilight Sparkle after she reads up on it. I asked Fable once to formalize fun and try to make a genetic algorithm for making a "fun" world to walk around it. On one hand, it didn't work, and failed in some weirdly dumb ways. But, it's initial responses were like [me paraphrasing from memory, aggregating some things that had a bit of prodding from me in between]</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Fun? That hasn't been exactly formalized already but probably what you want is one of the ways to formalize compression [link to some math theorem guy I whose name I forgot], and what a fun game is is a presentation of patterns that you keep needing to compress so you can think about them. You want something not too easy or hard to compress. When you run out of stuff to compress, the fun ends.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A naive failure mode would be "random noise that constantly slightly changes such that you never can fully compress it."
